The client needs to correctly cast the returned objects, unless the query parameters infer the return type.**Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Adds an interface and its implementation to the code.![image](images/box.jpg) These have to be updated with entity logic changes.![image](images/box.jpg) Has inherently leaky abstractions (see “Anti-Patterns,” later in this chapter).![image](images/box.jpg) Centralized reusable data access logic; however, coding reuse may be unrealistic or unlikely.![image](images/box.jpg) Potentially overlapping code with JPA’s facilities.![image](images/box.jpg) Probably the least awkward approach for proprietary data APIs.![image](images/exam.jpg)***The DAO pattern favors a stateless session bean for its implementation. The considerable benefits are as follows:***![image](images/box.jpg) ***A 1:1 thread relationship between the caller and the bean method, which means that the method is guaranteed to execute within a single thread***.![image](images/box.jpg) ***The transaction is invoked by default***.![image](images/box.jpg) ***Entities included in the transaction become attached, and they can be managed by the bean***.**Data Transfer Object**The Data Transfer Object (DTO or TO) makes data available across a service boundary. The DTO pattern is a copy of some underlying data. DTO patterns weigh the inherent problems of duplicating data against the expediency of delivering it where it is needed.**Problem**  Data Transfer Objects expose back-end data to a client or presentation tier.![image](images/box.jpg) This is problematic in J2EE (prior to JEE 5) for the following reasons:![image](images/box.jpg) Entity beans supported a remote capability.![image](images/box.jpg) The container supported two persistence management schemes.![image](images/box.jpg) The CMP 2.x standard did not support serialization.![image](images/box.jpg) Java EE addresses some issues, however:![image](images/box.jpg) JPA does not expose data.![image](images/box.jpg) Entities have attached and detached states.![image](images/box.jpg) Service-oriented applications should not see raw data.**Forces**![image](images/box.jpg) Need a common data interface for several client types.![image](images/box.jpg) Entity data is connected to a complex schema, but has to be exposed in a simple way.![image](images/box.jpg) Need multiple views of the same data in one domain.![image](images/box.jpg) Need to supply object-oriented data in a transferable form.![image](images/box.jpg) Metadata support needed to support UI development.**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) TransferObject is a simple POJO that can be Serializable or Externalizable (which is overkill in a single JVM deployment).![image](images/box.jpg) Getters can be used for each data column.![image](images/box.jpg) The component must create, populate, and return the DTO to the caller.**Strategies**![image](images/box.jpg) **Builder-style DTO** Each method that contributes to the object under construction returns a reference to the object. Using several methods all of which return a reference to the current object, it is possible to express a complex construction as a chain of methods in one statement. This technique helps to form what is known as a “fluent interface.”![image](images/box.jpg) **Builder-style immutable DTO** Declares required fields as final, which forces those values to be set in the constructor. This approach precludes the use of setter methods for those fields. Optional elements can then be added through builder methods.![image](images/box.jpg) **Client-specific DTO strategy** Uses annotations to supply metadata with the payload data. If one goal of the DTO is to support presentation of data, this strategy can help clients to convert data into parameters.Note that the Builder design pattern is a GoF structural pattern.**Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Transfer objects replicate data.![image](images/box.jpg) Transfer objects are per-value objects, whereas EntityManager objects are per-reference objects.![image](images/box.jpg) Consider using read-only entities.**Web Service Broker**Web Service Broker makes business services available as web services. The Web Service Broker pattern is carried over from previous versions of Java EE. Although in Java EE 6 it may be viewed as unnecessary, the pattern highlights a method for decorating an existing service so that it can be made available as a web service. The Web Service Broker is a like a protocol-compliant facade (see the GoF Adapter pattern).**Problem and Forces**  Exposing business services directly as web services may create the following conditions/issues:![image](images/box.jpg) Exposes methods that are not service oriented![image](images/box.jpg) Exposes methods that are too fine grained![image](images/box.jpg) Requires additional monitoring, logging, or security code![image](images/box.jpg) Requires reworking of several methods**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) The WebServiceBroker component can handle a web service request and invoke the Service component.![image](images/box.jpg) EndPointProcessor may receive the request and invoke the WebServiceBroker.![image](images/box.jpg) The WebServiceBroker component can be a stateless session bean or a POJO.**Strategies**![image](images/box.jpg) For custom XML messaging, write a custom EndPointProcessor component.![image](images/box.jpg) For Java API for XML Web Services, implement an EndPointProcessor via JAX-WS.![image](images/box.jpg) Use the Java API for RESTful Web Services (JAX-RS).![image](images/box.jpg) For Client-Broker, EJB 3.1 brokers can be injected into a servlet.**Consequences**  The advantage is:![image](images/box.jpg) The client and service protocols are separated.The disadvantages are:![image](images/box.jpg) Adds a layer of indirection.![image](images/box.jpg) Relies on a local interface. Business components with a remote interface may produce different side effects from thrown exceptions.![image](images/box.jpg) Web service protocols may increase network overhead.The following table is a list of scenarios addressed by an integration tier Java EE pattern.

| **SCENARIO & SOLUTION** |
| **Given Scenarios** | **Appropriate Integration Tier Java EE Pattern** |
| Integrate asynchronous messages with transaction services within the business container. | Asynchronous Resource Integrator |
| Reduce the amount of coupling and complexity between business and resource tiers, while still centralizing the access to the resource tier. | Data Access Object |
| Make data available across a service boundary. | Data Transfer Object |
| Make business services available as web Services. | Web Service Broker |

**Infrastructure Patterns**Infrastructure patterns provide a model approach to handling common requirements. They stress portability among Java EE servers and use dependency injection and annotations to reduce code plumbing. They are also useful for encapsulating artifacts of old APIs.Here are the Java EE patterns available for the infrastructure:![image](images/box.jpg) Bean Locator![image](images/box.jpg) Resource Binder![image](images/box.jpg) Service Starter![image](images/box.jpg) SingletonWe will now cover each of these infrastructure patterns.**Bean Locator**The Bean Locator pattern encapsulates JNDI services and provides them for non–Java EE clients.**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) Java Naming and Directory Interface (JNDI) API predates Java EE and Java SE 5.![image](images/box.jpg) A checked exception is used: `NamingException`.![image](images/box.jpg) An object is returned.![image](images/box.jpg) EJB 3.1 supports a global naming convention:![image](images/p325-01.jpg)![image](images/box.jpg) Neither form is convenient for clients.![image](images/box.jpg) Standalone clients and POJOs cannot use dependency injection (DI) as a workaround.**Forces**![image](images/box.jpg) JNDI details should always be hidden from users because:![image](images/box.jpg) Building global names is subject to typos.![image](images/box.jpg) JNDI method plumbing is awkward.![image](images/box.jpg) You want a lookup service that can execute either inside or outside the container.![image](images/box.jpg) You want a service that is client neutral.**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) Encapsulate the JNDI lookup code in a utility class.![image](images/box.jpg) Add a Builder-style `GlobalJNDIName` as a static inner class.**Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Construction of `GlobalJNDI` is tied to the Properties object.![image](images/box.jpg) File I/O may become a hotspot.![image](images/box.jpg) If testing reveals a hotspot, consider optimizations such as caching.![image](images/box.jpg) Solution is portable across Java EE–compliant environments.![image](images/box.jpg) Performance hits occur at bean creation time.![image](images/box.jpg) May not be noticeable by remote clients due to network latency.![image](images/box.jpg) The `computeBeanName()` method is slow on the first call.**Resource Binder**The Resource Binder pattern allows you to register custom resources with the application server.**Problem**  Each application server vendor provides the ability to register custom resources with the server. Although the Java EE specification does not offer a standardized approach, this feature is commonly available in the following ways:![image](images/box.jpg) Web-based administration utility with a drill-down or wizard-based process![image](images/box.jpg) Command-line interface with vendor-specific syntax**Forces**![image](images/box.jpg) You want a convenient way to register custom resources, as simple as the way Bean Locator works for lookup services.![image](images/box.jpg) The process should avoid proprietary application server bindings.![image](images/box.jpg) You want to inject custom resources into managed beans.![image](images/box.jpg) Resources are stateless and do not require life cycle management.**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) Implement a `CustomResource` as a POJO.![image](images/box.jpg) Inject the `CustomResource` into a `CustomResourceClient`.![image](images/box.jpg) Use a `ResourceBinder` to add the `CustomResource`.![image](images/box.jpg) Use annotations to assert the necessary order.**Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Injectability of custom resources reduces code plumbing.![image](images/box.jpg) This can increase server startup time.![image](images/box.jpg) Depends on resource startup requirements.![image](images/box.jpg) Should complete before business methods are called.![image](images/box.jpg) The SPI for resource binding is server specific.![image](images/box.jpg) Resource Binder relies on JNDI but should be tested with each server product.**Service Starter**The Service Starter pattern ensures that a session bean starts at deployment time.**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) Infrastructural beans frequently need to be initialized before a client calls business logic.![image](images/box.jpg) Prior to EJB 3.1, there was no standard way to initialize a bean upon deployment.**Forces**![image](images/box.jpg) Need a general approach to start services eagerly.![image](images/box.jpg) The approach must address stateless session beans, which are normally invoked on demand.**Solutions and Strategies**![image](images/box.jpg) For EJB 3.0:![image](images/box.jpg) Inject a bean into a servlet.![image](images/box.jpg) Call a bean starter method from the servlet’s `init()` method.![image](images/box.jpg) For EJB 3.1 and later:![image](images/box.jpg) Use a singleton bean with an `@PostConstruct` method.![image](images/box.jpg) If you want to load a bean before the Service Starter, use the `@DependsOn` annotation.**Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) There are various ways to implement:![image](images/box.jpg) Servlet 2.5![image](images/box.jpg) Servlet 3.0![image](images/box.jpg) Java EE 6 Singleton![image](images/box.jpg) Inconsistent schemes can cause startup code to execute multiple times.**Singleton**The Singleton implements a single instance of a resource, per JVM—and you almost always run more than one JVM in an enterprise application!**Problem**  Implementing a portable, homegrown Singleton pattern in an EJB container is not possible, because:![image](images/box.jpg) Thread control is out of the developer’s hands.![image](images/box.jpg) Thread pooling (size and behavior) is not defined by the specification.Therefore, it isn’t possible to build an efficient read-only cache under these circumstances.**Forces**![image](images/box.jpg) Build a Singleton that is portable across implementations.![image](images/box.jpg) The Singleton must be read-only or must provide synchronization control during updates.**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) EJB 3.1 standardizes a Singleton as a per-JVM instance.![image](images/box.jpg) A Singleton provides:![image](images/box.jpg) Shared state![image](images/box.jpg) Concurrency (managed by container)![image](images/box.jpg) A Singleton does not provide startup services.![image](images/box.jpg) For the most part, Singleton and Service Starter have the same underpinnings.**Strategies**![image](images/box.jpg) Gatekeeper![image](images/box.jpg) Enables legacy-resource throttling.![image](images/box.jpg) Suitable for single-node environments.![image](images/box.jpg) Useful if a JCA-based throttle is too heavy.![image](images/box.jpg) Caching Singleton![image](images/box.jpg) Can wrap an existing solution (for example, Ehcache, Memcached, or Hazelcast).![image](images/box.jpg) Can build your own.**Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Hard-wired solution for EJB 3.1 and beyond.![image](images/box.jpg) Can create a bottleneck with locking type:![image](images/box.jpg) `@ConcurrencyManagement(ConcurrencyManagementType.CONTAINER).`![image](images/box.jpg) `@Lock(LockType.WRITE).`![image](images/box.jpg) For read-only cache use `@Lock(LockType.READ).`![image](images/box.jpg) Can improve performance as part of a cache implementation, but this can also exhaust memory (work out limits with load/stress testing).![image](images/box.jpg) Does not satisfy high-availability requirements.The following table is a list of scenarios addressed by infrastructure Java EE patterns.

| **SCENARIO & SOLUTION** |
| **Given Scenarios** | **Appropriate Infrastructure Java EE Pattern** |
| Encapsulate JNDI services and also provide them for non–Java EE clients. | Bean Locator |
| Register custom resources with the application server. | Resource Binder |
| Ensure that a session bean starts at deployment time. | Service Starter |
| Implement a single instance of a resource per JVM. | Singleton |

**Interceptor Patterns**Interceptor patterns provide an aspect-oriented programming (AOP) approach to the Java EE platform. They are supported by session and message-driven beans and can be contained within the beans or as separate classes. They can be applied with annotations and configured with deployment descriptors. Intercepted classes or methods use annotations such as `@Interceptors(…).` Interceptor methods use `@AroundInvoke`, and interceptor timeouts use `@AroundTimeout`.Here are the Java EE Interceptor patterns:![image](images/box.jpg) Context Holder![image](images/box.jpg) Dependency Injection Extender![image](images/box.jpg) Payload Extractor![image](images/box.jpg) Thread TrackerWe will now cover each of these infrastructure patterns.**Context Holder**The Context Holder shares data among participants in a transaction.**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) In Java EE, there is no standard way to pass context-oriented information among participants in a transaction.**Forces**![image](images/box.jpg) Need to add data to a thread within the programming restrictions enforced by the container.![image](images/box.jpg) The target environment may use multiple thread pools, and `ThreadLocal` might not suffice.![image](images/box.jpg) A solution should not require changes to the parameter list of a bean.![image](images/box.jpg) A portable solution is ideal.**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) Java EE supports object type `TransactionSynchronizationRegistry` for each transaction.![image](images/box.jpg) In single-pool targets, `ThreadLocal<T>` is suitable.![image](images/box.jpg) The `@Resource` annotation is used for injection. The Service Facade is a good candidate.![image](images/box.jpg) All beans participating in the transaction will have access to the registry.**Strategies**![image](images/box.jpg) Use `TransactionSynchronizationRegistry:`![image](images/box.jpg) Most portable, but predates Java generics.![image](images/box.jpg) Requires transaction context.![image](images/box.jpg) Supports managed classes.![image](images/box.jpg) Use `ThreadLocal:`![image](images/box.jpg) Effective but less portable.![image](images/box.jpg) Requires 1:1 association between thread and method.![image](images/box.jpg) No support for dependency injection, but as such supports all participants.![image](images/box.jpg) Either strategy requires casting of fetched objects.**Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Context Holder is a cross-cutting approach that removes complexity from business services.![image](images/box.jpg) The `TransactionSynchronizationRegistry` strategy’s performance depends on the container implementation.![image](images/box.jpg) Performance of the `ThreadLocal` approach depends on the Java SE implementation.![image](images/box.jpg) `ThreadLocal` may break with complex pool implementations.**Dependency Injection Extender**The Dependency Injection Extender provides bootstrap capability for clients of other frameworks.**Problem**  Google’s Guice and SpringSource’s Spring components have different forms of injection control that are incompatible with Java EE’s dependency injection. They cannot be deployed directly as EJBs.**Forces**![image](images/box.jpg) You do not want to modify these components to integrate them into the Java EE framework.![image](images/box.jpg) EJBs should not be aware of any difference in these components.![image](images/box.jpg) You want these components to participate in an EJB’s transaction and security context.![image](images/box.jpg) You want to integrate legacy components (from other frameworks) into an EJB application.**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) Interceptors provide a reusable, cross-cutting service.![image](images/box.jpg) Use an interceptor to provide bootstrap support and injection services for legacy components.![image](images/box.jpg) The Injector instantiates its legacy component and uses `@PostConstruct` to bootstrap creation.![image](images/box.jpg) The Injector injects the instance on a method using `@AroundInvoke` and for the class using `@PostConstruct`.![image](images/box.jpg) EJB declares the Injector as an interceptor.**Strategies**![image](images/box.jpg) **Stateful session bean** Does not require a per-method approach to injection. Because of the one-to-one relationship between client and stateful session, simply include the injection of the legacy component and the bootstrap logic together in a method that is annotated with `@PostConstruct`.![image](images/box.jpg) **Stateless session bean** Typically has a many-to-one relationship with clients, so a method interceptor is required (unless all the methods are themselves idempotent).![image](images/box.jpg) **Transactional integration** The transaction context is already started by the container.**Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Implementation is portable across Java EE servers.![image](images/box.jpg) Interceptors and dependency injection use reflection, but database access latency can cancel out the hit to performance.![image](images/box.jpg) Testing the interceptor implementation in an EJB 3.0 container is very difficult; however, the embeddable EJB 3.1 container is useful for unit testing.**Payload Extractor**The Payload Extractor performs message typecasting and payload validation in a common class or utility.**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) The message type received by an MDB’s `onMessage()` method requires casting and testing checks that may fail or throw an exception.![image](images/box.jpg) There’s no way to tell message producers that they are sending messages of the wrong type or payload (that is, poison messages).![image](images/box.jpg) Rolling messages back may create a Hot Potato situation.![image](images/box.jpg) Dropped messages get lost in a fire-and-forget scenario.**Forces**![image](images/box.jpg) You want to redirect poison (wrong type or payload) messages to an appropriate “invalid message” destination.![image](images/box.jpg) You want to validate inbound message types before they reach the `onMessage()` method.![image](images/box.jpg) You want to validate the payload before it reaches the business logic.![image](images/box.jpg) You prefer a transaction-based solution.**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) Use interceptor to:![image](images/box.jpg) Validate the message type.![image](images/box.jpg) Extract the payload.![image](images/box.jpg) Delegate the payload to a business method.![image](images/box.jpg) Delegate invalid messages to a handler bean.![image](images/box.jpg) The handler bean sends invalid messages (type or payload), consumed from the destination, to a dead-letter queue or “invalid message” destination.**Strategies**![image](images/box.jpg) Handling errors:![image](images/box.jpg) Returning a consumed and unprocessed message has to be a reliable process.![image](images/box.jpg) The return mechanism (dead-letter queue and so on) should follow site practice.**Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Extractor logic is separated into its own class, providing reusable cross-cutting logic.![image](images/box.jpg) Easy to add or remove interceptors.![image](images/box.jpg) Unit-testing on the consumer method is much simpler.![image](images/box.jpg) Reflection is relatively expensive.**Thread Tracker**The Thread Tracker names threads, making them easier to identify during monitoring.**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) Session bean methods run in a single service thread.![image](images/box.jpg) Service threads are generic and are typically named by function and instance (for example, httpSSLWorkerThread-8080-3).![image](images/box.jpg) Correlating deadlocks or latency to a business method requires a clearer identification and association.**Forces**  A thread-tracker needs to be:![image](images/box.jpg) Applicable to any session bean![image](images/box.jpg) Straightforward to disable and undeploy![image](images/box.jpg) Portable across Java EE implementations**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) Intercept the business methods with a Tracker class.![image](images/box.jpg) Save the original thread name and rename it after a business purpose in a `try` block.![image](images/box.jpg) Restore the original thread name in a `finally` block.![image](images/box.jpg) Annotate interceptors for production use (or use deployment descriptors).**Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Solution may not be permitted by the target environment.![image](images/box.jpg) It is not supported by EJB spec.![image](images/box.jpg) The target could throw `AccessControlException` and/or `SecurityException`.![image](images/box.jpg) Glassfish v3 allows it.![image](images/box.jpg) Performance bottlenecks may occur on frequently called methods.![image](images/box.jpg) `InvocationContext` may be reflection based (which is slow).![image](images/box.jpg) Load testing is essential.![image](images/box.jpg) MDBs may be a better use case.![image](images/box.jpg) Monitoring solutions may not refresh thread names, but logging will be reliable.The following table is a list of challenges and scenarios addressed by the Interceptor Java EE patterns.

| **SCENARIO & SOLUTION** |
| **Given Scenarios** | **Appropriate Interceptor Java EE Pattern** |
| Share data among participants in a transaction in a portable way. | Context Holder |
| Provide bootstrap capability for clients of other frameworks (for example, Guice or Spring). | Dependency Injection Extender |
| Provide a common class for message typecasting and payload validation. | Payload Extractor |
| Make it easier to identify thread names for monitoring and logging. | Thread Tracker |

![image](images/exam.jpg)***Unlike earlier versions of the exam, knowledge of Java EE patterns is now required for Part I, and you will also find it necessary to use them in Parts II and III***.**CERTIFICATION OBJECTIVE 7.04****Identify the Use of the Law of Leaky Abstractions or a Specific Anti-Pattern in a Given Scenario**In this section we cover both the Law of Leaky Abstractions and the anti-patterns that will be tested in the exam.**Law of Leaky Abstractions**The Law of Leaky Abstractions is not an actual anti-pattern, but it is a common underlying cause of negative impacts on a system. It speaks to the fact that the larger and more complex abstractions become, the more likely they are to become leaky or error prone. A short quote from Joel Spolsky is often cited to sum this up: “All non-trivial abstractions, to some degree, are leaky.”Again, in summary:![image](images/box.jpg) The Law of Leaky Abstractions is not an actual anti-pattern but a common underlying cause.![image](images/box.jpg) Layered software forces one to rely on “high-level” abstractions.![image](images/box.jpg) Shielding or hiding complexity means some crucial details go unmentioned.![image](images/box.jpg) Lack of awareness of latent details leads to errors in practice.![image](images/exam.jpg)***In Part 1 of the exam, you are guaranteed to see a question on the Law of Leaky Abstractions!*****Anti-Patterns**Anti-patterns identify and document habits and practices that have a negative impact on system. The goal is that by studying anti-patterns, an architect can learn to avoid them and use preferred alternates. Many Java EE patterns are designed to avoid these habits and practices by documenting proven techniques. For the purposes of the examination and for an architect, anti-patterns are grouped into three types:![image](images/box.jpg) Integration tier anti-patterns![image](images/box.jpg) Business tier anti-patterns![image](images/box.jpg) Presentation tier anti-patterns**Integration Tier Anti-Patterns**Here is the list of integration tier anti-patterns:![image](images/box.jpg) Anemic Domain Model![image](images/box.jpg) Fat Message![image](images/box.jpg) Hot Potato![image](images/box.jpg) Monolithic Consumer![image](images/box.jpg) Not Pooling ConnectionsWe will now cover each of these integration tier anti-patterns.**Anemic Domain Model**The Anemic Domain Model is the anti-pattern that describes a model containing little (or no) business logic.**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) Business logic is implemented separately from the domain objects (or data) on which it operates.**Symptoms and Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Violates the principle of encapsulation![image](images/box.jpg) Forces public access to data, which tightens coupling![image](images/box.jpg) Requires an extra layer (DTOs) to negotiate multiple data consumers**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) Apply the PDO pattern to put data with its logic.![image](images/box.jpg) So many J2EE-based applications are bound to this model by the platform. Possibly a good reason to upgrade to a newer release of Java EE.**Fat Message**The Fat Message is the anti-pattern that describes a message containing a large amount of data in the payload.**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) The payload of an asynchronous message may be fat.**Symptoms and Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) High network latency.![image](images/box.jpg) Degradation of messaging server scalability.![image](images/box.jpg) Unmarshaling message payload is compute-intensive.**Solutions**![image](images/box.jpg) Review the Enterprise Integration Patterns (EIP) Splitter pattern.![image](images/box.jpg) Refactor the payload to include only data that is relevant to the business service.![image](images/box.jpg) Pass an ID or other reference instead of the large payload and let the recipient look up the data.**Hot Potato**The Hot Potato is the anti-pattern that describes when a messaging server continues to send a message that cannot be acknowledged by a consumer because the transaction keeps failing.**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) Messaging servers redeliver messages until they receive an acknowledgement from a consumer.![image](images/box.jpg) A transaction that rolls back on each attempt may not acknowledge the message, causing a continuous resend (loop).**Symptoms and Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) This is an infinite loop.![image](images/box.jpg) The system shows high utilization but low throughput.**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) If a transaction rolls back, acknowledge the message and forward it through another means (e-mail or to an error destination) for problem resolution.![image](images/box.jpg) Implement a resend threshold on the messaging server. When the threshold is reached, reassign the message to a dead-letter queue.**Monolithic Consumer**The Monolithic Consumer is the anti-pattern that describes when any business logic is placed in the consuming MDB instead of placing it in another class (e.g., another EJB or POJO).**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) Business logic is put in the `onMessage()` method of an MDB.![image](images/box.jpg) Logic is invoked only after a JMS message is received.**Symptoms and Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Clients cannot access business logic directly with a business method.![image](images/box.jpg) Unit testing the business logic is very difficult.**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) Confine the message-driven bean to:![image](images/box.jpg) Validating and processing the payload.![image](images/box.jpg) Calling the right business method.![image](images/box.jpg) Synchronous clients can also invoke the service (and unit tests too).![image](images/box.jpg) The Asynchronous Resource Integrator pattern embraces this approach.**Not Pooling Connections**The Not Pooling Connections anti-pattern describes when instead of utilizing a connection pool, a database connection is created and destroyed for each request.**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) Creating and destroying a database connection for each query is expensive (and slow).**Symptoms and Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Slow response times.![image](images/box.jpg) Poor scalability.![image](images/box.jpg) Driver initialization and lookup code is duplicated across the system.**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) Use a connection pool.![image](images/box.jpg) Use the DataSource interface.**Business Tier Anti-Patterns**Here is the list of business tier anti-patterns:![image](images/box.jpg) Cacheless Cow![image](images/box.jpg) Conversational Baggage![image](images/box.jpg) Golden Hammers of Session State![image](images/box.jpg) Local and Remote Interfaces Simultaneously![image](images/box.jpg) Sledgehammer for a FlyWe will now cover each of these business tier anti-patterns.**Cacheless Cow**The Cacheless Cow is the anti-pattern that describes when frequently retrieved, but seldom changed, resources are not placed in a cache.**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) When fairly static resources are repeatedly used without a caching strategy in place, then server and network resources are over-utilized (wasted).**Symptoms and Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Scalability is constrained by the per-request round trips between the container and the external resource.**Forces**![image](images/box.jpg) A stored result may suffice for as many or more requests than when new data is needed.![image](images/box.jpg) A reasonable size for a cache can be determined from normal application use.![image](images/box.jpg) There is enough memory to cache enough results to achieve a useful hit rate.![image](images/box.jpg) Fast concurrent reads can be satisfied while also protecting data as it is being modified.**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) Java `EE EntityManager` provides built-in caching.![image](images/box.jpg) Consider a cache for other service boundaries where objects get reused:![image](images/box.jpg) On a client-facing web server![image](images/box.jpg) Between clients and web tier components![image](images/box.jpg) Between web and business tier components![image](images/box.jpg) Consider vendor-provided caching solutions.![image](images/box.jpg) Consider cache-oriented patterns such as Command (GoF).**Conversational Baggage**The Conversational Baggage anti-pattern describes a scenario when a stateful session bean is utilized though a stateless session bean is sufficient.**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) By default, many tasks in enterprise applications use a stateful session.**Symptoms and Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Application scalability is reduced by increased container service demand.![image](images/box.jpg) Stateful services that do the same work in a stateless form appear more resource intensive.**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) Use a stateless session as the default model.![image](images/box.jpg) Justify a stateful session bean by listing the benefits that can be proven.**Golden Hammers of Session State**The Golden Hammers of Session State is the anti-pattern that describes when a single location approach is chosen to persist session state.**Problem**  There are well-known common places to store session state:![image](images/box.jpg) In the client tier with cookies![image](images/box.jpg) In the presentation tier with an `HttpSession` class![image](images/box.jpg) In the business tier with a stateful session bean**Symptoms and Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Committing to one location often requires workarounds.![image](images/box.jpg) The benefit of a central site for session data is arguable.![image](images/box.jpg) Performance varies for each application when using the site.**Solution**  When making a session state tool choice, consider the following:![image](images/box.jpg) Implications of losing the data. (Is it okay for this scenario?)![image](images/box.jpg) Location of the data to be used.![image](images/box.jpg) Size and type of the data.![image](images/box.jpg) Types of clients supported.**Local and Remote Interfaces Simultaneously**The Local and Remote Interfaces Simultaneously anti-pattern that describes when a local and remote interfaces are provided to both fine and course grained services.**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) Local interface methods may throw `RemoteException.`![image](images/box.jpg) Remote interfaces should only be coarse grained.**Symptoms and Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Different side effects between local and remote clients.![image](images/box.jpg) Poor performance if service is fine grained.**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) Separate coarse-grained from fine-grained services.![image](images/box.jpg) Allow remote access only to coarse-grained services.![image](images/box.jpg) Consider a Service Facade boundary for remote clients.![image](images/box.jpg) Allow local or no-view access only to fine-grained services.**Sledgehammer for a Fly**The Sledgehammer for a Fly anti-pattern describes when EJB, a technology that comes with extra overhead, is chosen over a simple POJO where only lightweight processing is the requirement.**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) EJB components are container managed, which implies some extra overhead.![image](images/box.jpg) For lightweight objects, this cost may outweigh its benefits.**Symptoms and Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Additional complexity; no apparent benefit![image](images/box.jpg) More code and objects than are needed![image](images/box.jpg) Possible impact on scaling and performance**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) If your code does not use the following container services, use a POJO:![image](images/box.jpg) Concurrency![image](images/box.jpg) Entity interaction![image](images/box.jpg) Interceptors![image](images/box.jpg) Life cycle management![image](images/box.jpg) Timers![image](images/box.jpg) Transaction management**Presentation Tier Anti-Patterns**This is the list of presentation tier anti-patterns:![image](images/box.jpg) Ad Lib TagLibs![image](images/box.jpg) Embedded Navigational Information![image](images/box.jpg) Including Common Functionality in Every ServletWe will now cover each of these presentation tier anti-patterns.**Ad Lib TagLibs**The Ad Lib TagLibs anti-pattern describes when logic is placed in a JSP custom tag library instead of within a POJO.**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) A model or controller’s logic may be moved from a JSP page into a custom tag.**Symptoms and consequences:**![image](images/box.jpg) Custom tags are difficult for rich clients to use.![image](images/box.jpg) Custom tags with model/controller logic may need many attributes.![image](images/box.jpg) Custom tags are an extra layer of development and maintenance compared to a POJO.**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) When creating custom tags, ask where the underlying logic will be easiest to maintain.**Embedded Navigational Information**The Embedded Navigational Information anti-pattern describes when resource locations are embedded into a JSP.**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) JSP pages often include URL and file references on the page.![image](images/box.jpg) JSP pages must be updated every time a filename or URL changes.**Symptoms and Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Users receive (404) Page Not Found errors until the JSP code is corrected.![image](images/box.jpg) The application flow is obscured.**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) Use the Front Controller pattern (a J2EE Pattern) and logical resource mapping; URLs are unlikely to change frequently.![image](images/box.jpg) Use custom tags to include fragments instead of the `jsp:include` action or the `<%@include>` directive. An include custom tag maps a generic attribute value, such as banner, to a specific resource.**Including Common Functionality in Every Servlet**The Including Common Functionality in Every Servlet anti-pattern describes when code is placed in the service methods of every servlet instead of in a single separate class.**Problem**![image](images/box.jpg) Every servlet invokes the same pre- and post-processing tasks in its service methods.**Symptoms and Consequences**![image](images/box.jpg) Tasks must be programmatically added and removed from multiple servlets.![image](images/box.jpg) The servlets have poor organization and structure.**Solution**![image](images/box.jpg) Place these tasks in filters.![image](images/box.jpg) Filters can be used with JSP pages and servlets.![image](images/box.jpg) Intercepting Filter pattern (a J2EE pattern) is a solution for this anti-pattern.The following table is a list of scenarios that signifies an anti-pattern.

| **SCENARIO &ANTI-PATTERN** |
| **Given Scenarios** | **Anti-Pattern** |
| Business logic is implemented separately from the domain objects (or data) on which it operates. | Anemic Domain Model |
| Payload of an asynchronous message may be fat (very large size), causing network latency and compute-intensive payload marshaling/unmarshaling. | Fat Message |
| Messaging system is showing high utilization with very little throughput. | Hot Potato |
| Unable to easily test (or independently call) business logic placed in an MDB. | Monolithic Consumer |
| Slow throughput of application that is creating and destroying a database connection for each query. | Not Pooling Connections |
| Static-like resources are repeatedly used and the server and network resources are shown as being over-utilized. | Cacheless Cow |
| Stateful session beans are being used by default and are showing more resource-intensive usage. | Conversational Baggage |
| By edict, all applications for a particular company use the same approach for storing session state, and performance is adversely affected or session state is occasionally lost. | Golden Hammers of Session State |
| Experiencing different side effects between local and remote clients (for example, poor performance for fine-grained services). | Local and Remote Interfaces Simultaneously |
| The application uses EJBs instead of POJOs for all business components. | Sledgehammer for a Fly |
| A model or controller’s logic has been moved from a JSP page into a custom tag, making it difficult to call from a rich client. | Ad Lib TagLibs |
| JSP pages must be updated every time a filename or URL changes. | Embedded Navigational Information |
| Every servlet is poorly structured, and each invokes the same processing tasks in its service methods. | Including Common Functionality in Every Servlet |

**CERTIFICATION SUMMARY**By studying this chapter, you now have an understanding of the Gang of Four’s design patterns and some introductory material on Java EE patterns. You should also understand which are the most appropriate patterns to use for given scenarios.![image](images/tick2.jpg) **TWO-MINUTE DRILL**Here are some of the key points from each certification objective in this chapter.**Select an Appropriate GoF Pattern for a Given Application Challenge**![image](images/box1.jpg) The Abstract Factory is most appropriate when the system needs to be independent of how its objects are created, composed, and represented.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Adapter is most appropriate when you want to utilize an existing class with an incompatible interface.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Bridge is most appropriate when you want to avoid a permanent binding between the functional abstraction and its implementation.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Builder is most appropriate when the algorithm for creating a complex object needs to be independent of the components that compose the object and how they are assembled.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Chain of Responsibility is most appropriate when more than one object can handle a request and the handler is unknown.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Command is most appropriate when you need to parameterize objects by an action to perform.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Composite is most appropriate when you want to represent a full or partial hierarchy of objects.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Decorator is most appropriate when you want to transparently and dynamically add responsibilities to objects without affecting other objects.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Facade is most appropriate when you want to provide a simpler interface to a more complex subsystem.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Factory Method is most appropriate when a class is not able to anticipate the class of objects it needs to create.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Flyweight is most appropriate when the application uses a considerable number of objects.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Interpreter is most appropriate when the grammar of the language is not complicated and efficiency is not a priority.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Iterator is most appropriate when access to a collection object is required without having to expose its internal representation.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Mediator is most appropriate when a set of objects communicates in complex but well-defined ways.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Memento is most appropriate when a snapshot containing enough information regarding the state of an object can be saved so that it can be restored to the complete state using the snapshot information later.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Observer is most appropriate when a change to an object requires changing other objects, and the number of objects that need to be changed is unknown.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Prototype is most appropriate when the classes to instantiate are to be specified at run time.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Proxy is most appropriate when you need a more versatile or sophisticated reference to an object, rather than a simple pointer.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Singleton is most appropriate when a single instance of a class is needed, and it must be accessible to clients from a well-known access point.![image](images/box1.jpg) The State is most appropriate when the behavior of an object depends on its state and it must be able to change its behavior at run time according to the new state.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Strategy is most appropriate when multiple classes differ only in their behavior.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Template Method is most appropriate when you want to implement the nonvarying parts of an algorithm in a single class and the varying parts of the algorithm in subclasses.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Visitor is most appropriate when an object structure contains many objects with differing interfaces and you need to perform operations on these objects in a way that depends on their concrete classes.**Identify a Gang of Four (GoF) Design Pattern, Using a Description of Its Features**Review the Gang of Four (Gamma et al.) diagrams and associated descriptions that appear earlier in the chapter.**Demonstrate Knowledge of Java EE Design Patterns**![image](images/box1.jpg) The Fluid Logic is most appropriate when you want to incorporate “change on demand” scripted business logic into a service.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Gateway is most appropriate when you want to directly expose Persistent Domain Objects (PDOs) to the presentation tier or client.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Persistent Domain Object is most appropriate when you want to maintain OO principles by encapsulating logic with the data on which it operates.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Service Facade is most appropriate when you want to expose a coarsegrained (higher-level) business service interface.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Service is most appropriate when you want to decompose business requirements into technical implementations with a restricted view.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Asynchronous Resource Integrator is most appropriate when you want to integrate asynchronous messages with transaction services within the business container.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Data Access Object is most appropriate when you want to reduce the amount of coupling and complexity between business and resource tiers, while still centralizing the access to resource tier.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Data Transfer Object is most appropriate when you want to make data available across a service boundary.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Web Service Broker is most appropriate when you want to make business services available as web services.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Bean Locator is most appropriate when you want to encapsulate JNDI services and to also provide them for non–Java EE clients.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Resource Binder is most appropriate when you want to register custom resources with the application server.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Service Starter is most appropriate when you want to ensure that a session bean starts at deployment time.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Singleton is most appropriate when you want to implement a single instance of a resource per JVM.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Context Holder is most appropriate when you want to share data among participants in a transaction in a portable way.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Dependency Injection Extender is most appropriate when you want to provide bootstrap capability for clients of other frameworks (for example, Guice or Spring).![image](images/box1.jpg) The Payload Extractor is most appropriate when you want to provide a common class for message typecasting and payload validation.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Thread Tracker is most appropriate when you want to make it easier to identify thread names for monitoring and logging.**Identify the Use of the Law of Leaky Abstractions or a Specific Anti-Pattern in a Given Scenario**Review the Law of Leaky Abstractions and anti-pattern descriptions that appear earlier in the chapter.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Law of Leaky Abstractions is a common underlying cause of negative impacts on a system. Larger and more complex abstractions become increasingly more likely to become leaky or error prone. In short, all non-trivial abstractions are leaky.![image](images/box1.jpg) Integration tier anti-patterns:![image](images/box1.jpg) The Anemic Domain Model anti-pattern is when business logic is implemented separately from the domain objects (or data) on which it operates.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Fat Message anti-pattern is when the payload of an asynchronous message may be fat.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Hot Potato anti-pattern is when messaging servers redeliver messages until they receive an acknowledgement from a consumer. A transaction that rolls back on each attempt may not acknowledge the message, causing a continuous resend (loop).![image](images/box1.jpg) The Monolithic Consumer anti-pattern involves putting business logic in the `onMessage ( )` method of an MDB and logic is invoked only after a JMS message is received.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Not Pooling Connections anti-pattern is when a database connection is created and destroyed for each query (expensive and slow).![image](images/box1.jpg) Business tier anti-patterns:![image](images/box1.jpg) The Cacheless Cow anti-pattern is when fairly static resources are repeatedly used without a caching strategy in place and the server and network resources are over-utilized (wasted).![image](images/box1.jpg) The Conversational Baggage anti-pattern is when, by default, many tasks in enterprise applications use a stateful session.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Golden Hammers of Session State anti-pattern is when session state is stored in the wrong tier for the required functionality.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Local and Remote Interfaces Simultaneously anti-pattern is when the local interface methods throw `RemoteException`, and remote interfaces should only be coarse grained.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Sledgehammer for a Fly anti-pattern is when EJB components are used instead of POJOs for lightweight processing. EJB components are container-managed, implying extra overhead. The extra overhead is not needed for lightweight processing objects.![image](images/box1.jpg) Presentation tier anti-patterns:![image](images/box1.jpg) The Ad Lib TagLibs anti-pattern is when a model or controller’s logic contained in a JSP page should be moved into a custom tag.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Embedded Navigational Information anti-pattern is when JSP pages include URL and file references and therefore must be updated every time a filename or URL changes.![image](images/box1.jpg) The Including Common Functionality in Every Servlet anti-pattern is when every servlet invokes the same pre- and post-processing tasks in its service methods.**SELF TEST**The following questions will help you measure your understanding of the material presented in this chapter.
